Here the determinant is enclosed on each individual rectangle before the bounds are combined; it is not computed from separately combined entry ranges. The global derivative bounds, rounded outwards for display, are $$L_1\le7.4636170,\qquad L_2\le2.5091315,\qquad
 L_3\le143.7890461,\qquad L_4\le315.1489689.$$ These derivative numbers are projections of the computed hulls, not separate executable threshold assertions. For the identified sources, under the documented runtime assumptions, the completed run verifies the listed finite assertions using uncoarsened interval endpoints. Proposition <a href="#comp:finite-soundness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:finite-soundness">19</a> explains why these assertions imply the required bounds for the exact infinite-mode functions: the interval formulas and range-coverage arguments identify the quantities enclosed, while the analytic truncation estimates supply the omitted modes. Neither the rounded table nor the execution record alone supplies those mathematical correspondences.

<a id="stmt-comp-scalar"></a>
<div id="comp:scalar" class="theorem">

**Theorem 20** (Scalar inequalities from the finite verification). *The exact constants and functions of <a href="#cal:constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cal:constants">[cal:constants]</a> and <a href="#cal:primitive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cal:primitive">[cal:primitive]</a> satisfy $$K<0,\qquad 2g_d(x)\ge K\quad(d\ge b,\ x\ge0),\qquad
 \int_0^\infty\max\{0,-G(d,s)\}\,\mathrm ds\le-K
                                             \quad(d\ge b).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-comp-scalar"><summary>Proof of stmt-comp-scalar</summary>

*Proof.* The documented finite verification and the correspondences just established supply the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#comp:finite-soundness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:finite-soundness">19</a>. The first assertion is part of <a href="#comp:tail-signs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:tail-signs">[comp:tail-signs]</a>. The outer-grid assertion and <a href="#comp:inner-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:inner-conclusion">[comp:inner-conclusion]</a> cover all of $\mathcal D$. For $d\in[b,.697]$ the positive tail makes the primitive increasing after $x=.65$, so the primitive bound extends to every $x\ge0$. For $d\ge.697$, $G(d,s)>0$ for all $s\ge0$, whence $g_d(x)\ge0>K/2$. The negative-part estimate follows from <a href="#comp:negative-assertion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:negative-assertion">[comp:negative-assertion]</a> on the rectangle; its integrand vanishes outside the rectangle by <a href="#comp:positive-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:positive-tail">[comp:positive-tail]</a>. These are exactly <a href="#cal:scalar-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cal:scalar-bounds">[cal:scalar-bounds]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:conclusion"></a>
### The finite-torus and whole-plane bounds

We now apply the geometric comparison to the certified scalar bounds, then pass from square tori to the plane. The square-torus conclusion of Proposition <a href="#dual:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dual:assembly">14</a> already incorporates the separation of minimizers and the passage to arbitrary configurations.

<details id="proof-stmt-comp-scalar-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-comp-scalar</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:torus">2</a>.* The radial data of Section <a href="#sec:calibration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:calibration">[sec:calibration]</a> satisfy <a href="#dual:radial-origin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dual:radial-origin">[dual:radial-origin]</a>–<a href="#dual:radial-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dual:radial-sum">[dual:radial-sum]</a> with lower distance $d_0=b=28209/100000$. Theorem <a href="#comp:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:scalar">20</a> gives $K<0$ and both bounds in <a href="#cal:scalar-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cal:scalar-bounds">[cal:scalar-bounds]</a>. By Lemma <a href="#dual:signed-endpoints" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dual:signed-endpoints">13</a> at $d_0=b$ and the primitive identity <a href="#cal:sector-primitive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cal:sector-primitive">[cal:sector-primitive]</a>, these bounds give <a href="#dual:sector-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dual:sector-input">[dual:sector-input]</a> for every sector with $d\geq b$, including the signed endpoint cases.

Since $b<1/(2\sqrt\pi)=d_*$, as follows from $\pi<3.1416$, the square-torus conclusion of Proposition <a href="#dual:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dual:assembly">14</a> applies. For every $n\geq2$ and every configuration of $n$ distinct points it gives, using the exact triangular calibration <a href="#cal:triangular-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cal:triangular-energy">[cal:triangular-energy]</a>, $$\mathcal W_n(h)\geq n(6K+\lambda+2\pi\mu)=nW(E_\triangle).$$ No separation hypothesis is imposed on this configuration. The same proposition gives the energy penalty $n|c|^2/2$ for a constant harmonic component and preserves the bound under a fixed orthogonal rotation. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-comp-scalar-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-comp-scalar</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* The periodic triangular field belongs to the admissible class and has finite energy by Lemmas <a href="#red:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:local">4</a> and <a href="#red:periodic-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:periodic-average">6</a>. Thus $C=W(E_\triangle)$ is a finite real number. Apply Proposition <a href="#red:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:reduction">7</a> to Theorem <a href="#thm:torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:torus">2</a> with this $C$. It gives the lower bound for every field in the stated admissible class and for each fixed cutoff family <a href="#eq:cutoffs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cutoffs">[eq:cutoffs]</a>. The triangular field attains the lower bound. ◻

</details>

The comparison establishes the minimum of this logarithmic renormalized energy. It does not classify all configurations having that energy per unit area. In particular it supplies no uniqueness or defect-exclusion assertion.

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
#### The spherical logarithmic-energy constant

<details id="proof-stmt-comp-scalar-4"><summary>Proof of stmt-comp-scalar</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:bhs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:bhs">3</a>.* Write $\mathcal A_m^{\mathrm{BS}}$ and $W_{\mathrm{ball}}$ for the admissible class and ball-cutoff energy of Bétermin and Sandier (Bétermin and Sandier 2018, Definitions 2.1–2.4) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[BeterminSandier2018](#ref-BeterminSandier2018)]. Their fields satisfy $\operatorname{div}E=2\pi(\nu_\Lambda-m)$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{curl}}E=0$, with the same charge-growth condition and local puncture functional as here. At $m=1$ the admissible class is our $\mathcal A_1$; the local integrability conventions agree by Lemma <a href="#red:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:local">4</a>. The outer average, however, is over centered balls rather than squares.

<a id="eq:bhs-minimum-normalization"></a>
The component rotation $j\mapsto F=(j_2,-j_1)$ is a bijection from $\mathcal C_1$ to $\mathcal A_{1/(2\pi)}^{\mathrm{BS}}$ and preserves the local energy. Thus <a href="#red:M" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:M">[red:M]</a> identifies $M$ with $\min_{\mathcal A_{1/(2\pi)}^{\mathrm{BS}}}W_{\mathrm{ball}}$. The scaling formula (Bétermin and Sandier 2018, equation (2.4)) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[BeterminSandier2018](#ref-BeterminSandier2018)] gives $$\label{eq:bhs-minimum-normalization}
\min_{\mathcal A_1^{\mathrm{BS}}}W_{\mathrm{ball}}
=2\pi\left(M-\frac14\log(2\pi)\right).$$ Lemma <a href="#red:plane-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:plane-bound">5</a> bounds every square-cutoff energy $W(E)$ below by the right-hand side. Conversely, periodic averaging applied to the scaled square-periodic sequence in the proof of Proposition <a href="#red:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:reduction">7</a> shows that its square-cutoff energies converge to that value. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> therefore identifies it with $W(E_\triangle)$. This uses the common ball and square minimum in <a href="#red:M" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:M">[red:M]</a>, not equality of those two energies for an arbitrary field. Lemma <a href="#red:periodic-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:periodic-average">6</a> does give equality for the periodic field $E_\triangle$, so this covolume-one triangular field attains the minimum in <a href="#eq:bhs-minimum-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bhs-minimum-normalization">[eq:bhs-minimum-normalization]</a>. Its value also agrees with the periodic lattice notation in Bétermin and Sandier: replacing their gradient $\nabla H_\Lambda$ by the admissible field $-\nabla H_\Lambda$ leaves the local squared-norm functional unchanged.

Theorem 1.5 of (Bétermin and Sandier 2018) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[BeterminSandier2018](#ref-BeterminSandier2018)] gives the form of <a href="#eq:bhs-asymptotic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bhs-asymptotic">[eq:bhs-asymptotic]</a> with $C$ in place of $C_{\mathrm{BHS}}$, where $$C=\frac1\pi\min_{\mathcal A_1^{\mathrm{BS}}}W_{\mathrm{ball}}
       +\frac12\log\pi+\log2,$$ and states that $C=C_{\mathrm{BHS}}$ exactly when the density-one triangular lattice attains this minimum. Its energy is precisely the ordered-pair sum on the unit sphere in $\mathbb R^3$ used here. In particular, the $-n(n-1)\log2$ term in its passage from the radius-$1/2$ sphere to the unit sphere contributes both $-n^2\log2$ and $+n\log2$; no further pair-counting or radius factor is needed. The equality clause now gives <a href="#eq:bhs-constant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bhs-constant">[eq:bhs-constant]</a> and proves the corollary. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:scientific-support"></a>
### Supplementary coefficient and tail estimates

The proof uses the coefficient enclosures and omitted-mode estimates in Sections <a href="#tail:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tail:section">6</a> and <a href="#sec:computation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:computation">7</a>. This appendix records an independent integer recurrence for the same finite coefficient sums and alternative exact bounds for the same omitted-mode series. These arguments are supplementary: they do not add a hypothesis to the scalar theorem or replace the analytic derivative bounds and finite retained-mode premises used there.

<a id="supp:coefficient-recurrence"></a>
#### An independent recurrence for the lattice coefficients

<a id="supp:real-recurrence"></a>
Fix integers $(n,q)\ne(0,0)$ and write $$A=2n+q,\qquad N=n^2+nq+q^2,\qquad
 \zeta=A+i\sqrt3q,\qquad w=\frac{\zeta}{2}.$$ Thus $w=n+q/2+i\sqrt3q/2$, $N=|w|^2>0$, and $|\zeta|^2=4N$. A point of the covolume-one triangular lattice is $aw$, where $a=\sqrt{2/\sqrt3}$. For $m\geq0$, put $$r_m(n,q)=\operatorname{Re}(\zeta^{6m}).$$ The two numbers $u=\zeta^6$ and $v=\overline{\zeta}^{6}$ have $$u+v=2r_1,\qquad uv=(4N)^6,$$ and hence satisfy $X^2-2r_1X+(4N)^6=0$. Multiplying this equation by $u^{m-1}$ and $v^{m-1}$, respectively, and adding gives the integer recurrence $$\begin{split}
 r_0&=1,\\
 r_1&=A^6-45A^4q^2+135A^2q^4-27q^6,\\
 r_{m+1}&=2r_1r_m-(4N)^6r_{m-1}\qquad(m\geq1).
 \end{split}
 \label{supp:real-recurrence}$$ Here all the quantities on the right are integers. This proves the recurrence for every individual lattice point, without a numerical approximation to a complex power.

<a id="supp:reciprocal-power"></a>
The binomial theorem identifies its numerator exactly. Since only even powers of $i\sqrt3q$ have nonzero real part, <a id="supp:binomial-numerator"></a>$$r_m(n,q)=\sum_{j=0}^{3m}(-1)^j\binom{6m}{2j}
                 A^{6m-2j}(3q^2)^j.
 \label{supp:binomial-numerator}$$ This is the numerator in <a href="#comp:coefficient-summand" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:coefficient-summand">[comp:coefficient-summand]</a>, after writing $q$ for its second integer coordinate. Indeed, $w^{-6m}=\overline{w}^{6m}/N^{6m}$, and therefore $$\operatorname{Re}(w^{-6m})
 =\frac{r_m(n,q)}{2^{6m}N^{6m}}
 =\frac{r_m(n,q)}{(64N^6)^m}.
 \label{supp:reciprocal-power}$$ Thus the recurrence and the binomial expression agree for every point and every mode, not only for a selected finite list of comparisons.

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
###### Grouping by the norm.

<a id="supp:norm-grouping"></a>
For a positive integer $D$, let $$\mathcal Q_D=\{(n,q)\in\mathbb Z^2:\ |n|,|q|\leq D,\ (n,q)\ne(0,0)\},
 \qquad
 R_{m,N}(D)=\sum_{\substack{(n,q)\in\mathcal Q_D\\n^2+nq+q^2=N}}
                         r_m(n,q).$$ After forming the numerator for each point, one may combine the equal denominators in <a href="#supp:reciprocal-power" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="supp:reciprocal-power">[supp:reciprocal-power]</a>: $$\sum_{(n,q)\in\mathcal Q_D}\operatorname{Re}(w^{-6m})
 =\sum_{N\geq1}\frac{R_{m,N}(D)}{(64N^6)^m}.
 \label{supp:norm-grouping}$$ Only finitely many positive norms occur. It is important that the recurrence precede this grouping. For fixed $D$, summing <a href="#supp:real-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="supp:real-recurrence">[supp:real-recurrence]</a> over a norm shell gives $$R_{m+1,N}(D)
 =2\sum_{\substack{(n,q)\in\mathcal Q_D\\n^2+nq+q^2=N}}
       r_1(n,q)r_m(n,q)-(4N)^6R_{m-1,N}(D).$$ In general the first sum cannot be replaced by one value of $r_1$ times $R_{m,N}(D)$. For example, $(7,0)$ and $(5,3)$ both have $N=49$, but their respective values of $r_1$ are $7\,529\,536$ and $-4\,912\,064$. One may apply the recurrence either pointwise or within groups having the same pair $(N,r_1)$, and only then combine all terms of a common norm.

For the finite coefficient enclosures, take $D=256$ when $m=1$ and $D=48$ when $2\leq m\leq38$. The omitted dimensionless sum has absolute value at most $$E_{m,D}=8(4/3)^{3m}\frac{D^{2-6m}}{6m-2}$$ by <a href="#tail:coefficient-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:coefficient-square">[tail:coefficient-square]</a>. The coefficient $c_m$ is consequently enclosed by adding $[-E_{m,D},E_{m,D}]$ to the finite sum in <a href="#supp:norm-grouping" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="supp:norm-grouping">[supp:norm-grouping]</a> and then multiplying by the positive factor $a^{-6m}/(6m)$. The square and its omitted tail are unchanged by grouping.

<a id="pm-h-49"></a>
###### Complex pairs and interval containment.

The finite calculation may instead keep both real components. Write $$\zeta^6=r_1+i\sqrt3\,s_1,\qquad
 s_1=6A^5q-60A^3q^3+54Aq^5.$$ The multiplication rule $$(x+i\sqrt3y)(x'+i\sqrt3y')
 =(xx'-3yy')+i\sqrt3(xy'+yx')$$ shows that, starting from $(x_0,y_0)=(1,0)$, $$(x_{m+1},y_{m+1})
 =(x_mr_1-3y_ms_1,\ x_ms_1+y_mr_1)$$ represents $\zeta^{6(m+1)}$ exactly. In particular $x_m=r_m$ and its real component obeys <a href="#supp:real-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="supp:real-recurrence">[supp:real-recurrence]</a>. This is the complex-pair recursion used in the finite calculation, again applied per point before norm grouping. The half square $n>0$, or $n=0$ and $q>0$, contains one point of each pair $(n,q),(-n,-q)$. Because $6m$ is even, the two real numerators agree, which justifies doubling each half-square contribution.

For completeness, grouping also explains an interval containment relation. At fixed denominator $Q=2^{144}$, let $$\mathcal I_Q(x)=
 \left[\frac{\lfloor Qx\rfloor}{Q},
       \frac{\lceil Qx\rceil}{Q}\right]
 \qquad(x\in\mathbb Q).$$ For any finite rational list $x_1,\ldots,x_h$, the elementary inequalities $$\left\lfloor\sum_jQx_j\right\rfloor\geq\sum_j\lfloor Qx_j\rfloor,
 \qquad
 \left\lceil\sum_jQx_j\right\rceil\leq\sum_j\lceil Qx_j\rceil$$ give <a id="supp:interval-grouping"></a>$$\mathcal I_Q\left(\sum_jx_j\right)
       \subseteq\sum_j\mathcal I_Q(x_j).
 \label{supp:interval-grouping}$$ Apply this to the exact rationals in each norm shell. Converting the shell sum once and then summing shells is contained in the enclosure obtained by converting every point separately. Adding the same outward tail interval and multiplying by the same positive scaling enclosure preserve containment, because interval addition, multiplication, and outward rounding are monotone under inclusion. This statement assumes the same primitive rounding rule, tail enclosure, and scaling enclosure on both sides; exact algebra alone does not assert endpoint identity or containment between different choices of those enclosures.

<a id="supp:tail-bounds"></a>
#### Alternative exact bounds for the omitted modes

<a id="supp:initial-ratio-bound"></a>
For the allowed indices $k=234,240,\ldots$, split the weighted summand $(k+1)^4w_k$ of <a href="#tail:w-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:w-definition">[tail:w-definition]</a> into four positive rationals: $$\begin{aligned}
 t_1(k)&=10^6\,30(k+1)^4k^2(13/20)^k,\\
 t_2(k)&=10^6(3\cdot10^6)(k+1)^4k^3(1/2)^k,\\
 t_3(k)&=10^6\,5600(k+1)^4k^5(3/5)^k,\\
 t_4(k)&=10^6(7\cdot10^{11})(k+1)^4k^2(289/500)^k.
\end{aligned}$$ Then $$\mathfrak a=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^4t_i(234+6j).$$ This is the same $\mathfrak a$ as in <a href="#tail:weighted-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:weighted-sum">[tail:weighted-sum]</a>, distinct from the lattice scale $a$. If $$(p_i,\beta_i)=(2,13/20),(3,1/2),(5,3/5),(2,289/500),$$ in the order $i=1,2,3,4$, the exact consecutive ratios are <a id="supp:component-ratios"></a>$$\rho_i(k)=\frac{t_i(k+6)}{t_i(k)}
 =\left(\frac{k+7}{k+1}\right)^4
  \left(\frac{k+6}{k}\right)^{p_i}\beta_i^6.
 \label{supp:component-ratios}$$ Both rational factors decrease for $k>0$, so each $\rho_i(k)$ decreases. The four initial ratios, with no rounding, are $$\begin{array}{c|c}
 i&\rho_i(234)\\ \hline
 1&(241/235)^4(40/39)^2(13/20)^6\\
 2&(241/235)^4(40/39)^3(1/2)^6\\
 3&(241/235)^4(40/39)^5(3/5)^6\\
 4&(241/235)^4(40/39)^2(289/500)^6.
 \end{array}$$ Here is an exact simultaneous check that all four are less than $1/10$. The integer comparisons $$\begin{aligned}
 9\cdot241^4=30\,360\,623\,049
   &<30\,498\,006\,250=10\cdot235^4,\\
 7\cdot40^5=716\,800\,000
   &<721\,793\,592=8\cdot39^5,\\
 800\cdot13^6=3\,861\,447\,200
   &<4\,032\,000\,000=63\cdot20^6
\end{aligned}$$ give, since $p_i\leq5$ and $0<\beta_i\leq13/20$, $$\rho_i(234)
 \leq(241/235)^4(40/39)^5(13/20)^6
 <\frac{10}{9}\frac87(13/20)^6<\frac1{10}
 \quad(1\leq i\leq4).
 \label{supp:initial-ratio-bound}$$

<a id="pm-h-51"></a>
###### Three geometric bounds.

Set $T_0=\sum_{i=1}^4t_i(234)$. One convenient entirely integer form of its exact comparison is obtained by putting $$\begin{aligned}
 C_{234}={}&30\cdot234^2\cdot325^{234}
 +3\cdot10^6\cdot234^3\cdot250^{234}\\
 &+5600\cdot234^5\cdot300^{234}
 +7\cdot10^{11}\cdot234^2\cdot289^{234}.
\end{aligned}$$ The common denominator $500^{234}$ gives $$T_0=\frac{10^6\,235^4 C_{234}}{500^{234}},\qquad
 10^{29}235^4 C_{234}<156\cdot500^{234}.$$ The displayed integer inequality is exactly $T_0<156/10^{23}$. In particular, <a id="supp:first-sum-bound"></a>$$\frac{10}{9}T_0<\frac{1560}{9\cdot10^{23}}
       <\frac2{10^{21}},
 \label{supp:first-sum-bound}$$ where the last comparison reduces to $1560<1800$.

<a id="supp:geometric-bounds"></a>
Let $\rho_{\max}=\max_{1\leq i\leq4}\rho_i(234)$, and define three exact rational upper bounds $$A_{\rm coarse}=\frac{10}{9}T_0,\qquad
 A_{\rm common}=\frac{T_0}{1-\rho_{\max}},\qquad
 A_{\rm component}=\sum_{i=1}^4
                \frac{t_i(234)}{1-\rho_i(234)}.$$ Decreasing ratios imply $t_i(234+6j)\leq t_i(234)\rho_i(234)^j$. Summing these geometric majorants and using <a href="#supp:initial-ratio-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="supp:initial-ratio-bound">[supp:initial-ratio-bound]</a> proves $$\mathfrak a\leq A_{\rm component}
 \leq A_{\rm common}<A_{\rm coarse}<\frac2{10^{21}}.
 \label{supp:geometric-bounds}$$ The second inequality holds because $1-\rho_i(234)\geq1-\rho_{\max}>0$. The common-ratio estimate keeps only the largest initial ratio, whereas the componentwise estimate retains each one; both refine the coarse factor $10/9$.

<a id="pm-h-52"></a>
###### An exact partial sum and a positive remainder bound.

<a id="supp:partial-tail-bound"></a>
There are exactly $62$ allowed indices from $234$ through $600$, because $600=234+6\cdot61$. Define the finite rational sum <a id="supp:partial-600"></a>$$P_{600}=\sum_{i=1}^4\sum_{j=0}^{61}t_i(234+6j)
 \label{supp:partial-600}$$ and the positive rational number <a id="supp:remainder-606"></a>$$R_{606}=\sum_{i=1}^4\frac{t_i(606)}{1-\rho_i(606)},\qquad
 \rho_i(606)=\left(\frac{613}{607}\right)^4
             \left(\frac{102}{101}\right)^{p_i}\beta_i^6.
 \label{supp:remainder-606}$$ Every numerator is positive and $0<\rho_i(606)<\rho_i(234)<1/10$, so $R_{606}>0$. Starting the geometric estimate at the next mode $606$, rather than at the last included mode $600$, gives $$P_{600}<\mathfrak a\leq P_{600}+R_{606}.
 \label{supp:partial-tail-bound}$$ The first inequality is strict because all subsequent summands are positive. The number $R_{606}$ is an upper bound for the remainder, not an assertion that the remainder equals it.

This refinement is no larger than the componentwise bound. For the $i$-th component put $r=\rho_i(234)$. Then $t_i(606)\leq t_i(234)r^{62}$ and $\rho_i(606)\leq r$, while each term of its partial sum is at most $t_i(234)r^j$. Consequently $$\sum_{j=0}^{61}t_i(234+6j)
       +\frac{t_i(606)}{1-\rho_i(606)}
 \leq t_i(234)\left(\sum_{j=0}^{61}r^j+
                         \frac{r^{62}}{1-r}\right)
 =\frac{t_i(234)}{1-r}.$$ Summing over $i$ places $P_{600}+R_{606}$ below $A_{\rm component}$ in <a href="#supp:geometric-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="supp:geometric-bounds">[supp:geometric-bounds]</a>. These finite bounds are exact rationals; a rounded display that makes $P_{600}$ and $P_{600}+R_{606}$ look equal does not change <a href="#supp:partial-tail-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="supp:partial-tail-bound">[supp:partial-tail-bound]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-53"></a>
###### Rational propagation to the radial errors.

<a id="supp:rational-error-coefficient"></a>
The refined bounds can be substituted directly into the propagation estimates of Section <a href="#tail:propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tail:propagation">6.5</a>. They do not replace its analytic mode bounds or the finite premises <a href="#tail:finite-core-premise" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:finite-core-premise">[tail:finite-core-premise]</a>: the retained second-order $B_1$ norm is still bounded by $6000$, and the weighted third-order $D_i$ norm by $2\cdot10^6$. Recall also that <a id="supp:W-bound"></a>$$W_*=4\sum_{k=234,240,\ldots}w_k
 \leq4\sum_{k=234,240,\ldots}(k+1)^4w_k=4\mathfrak a,
 \label{supp:W-bound}$$ so replacing $W_*$ by $4\mathfrak a$ is an upper bound, not an identity. Collecting the integral and mode-norm contributions in <a href="#tail:f-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:f-estimate">[tail:f-estimate]</a> and using $\pi<22/7$ gives the rational expression, for $A,W\geq0$, <a id="supp:rational-error-expression"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal F(A,W)={}&2\frac{22}{7}(3W+16A)\notag\\
 &+\frac12\bigl(60000W+8A+12(2\cdot10^6)W
       +48(6000+W)A\notag\\
 &\hspace{30mm}+8(2\cdot10^6)A+16A^2\bigr).
 \label{supp:rational-error-expression}
\end{aligned}$$ The terms are respectively the source integral, the $S_2$ error, the linear $D_i$ error, the retained and omitted mixed $D_iH_{k_i}$ errors, and the quadratic $D_iD_j$ error. The bound $\pi<22/7$ and <a href="#tail:f-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:f-estimate">[tail:f-estimate]</a> give $|f_0-\widetilde f_0|_{2,t}\leq\mathcal F(\mathfrak a,W_*)$. Every coefficient of $\mathcal F$ is nonnegative. Thus any one of the rational upper bounds $A$ above, for which $\mathfrak a\leq A$, satisfies $$|f_0-\widetilde f_0|_{2,t}
 \leq\mathcal F(\mathfrak a,W_*)\leq\mathcal F(A,4A).$$ Direct collection of the rational terms gives the exact identity $$\mathcal F(A,4A)=A\bigl(56\,264\,180+104A\bigr).
 \label{supp:rational-error-coefficient}$$ For example, the source term contributes $176A$; the remaining linear terms contribute $56\,264\,004A$, and the quadratic terms contribute $104A^2$.

Put $A_0=2/10^{21}$. Each rational upper bound for $\mathfrak a$ above is less than $A_0$, and the coefficient in <a href="#supp:rational-error-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="supp:rational-error-coefficient">[supp:rational-error-coefficient]</a> increases with $A$. The exact comparison $$56\,264\,180+\frac{208}{10^{21}}
 <56\,264\,181<6\cdot10^7$$ therefore supplies the claimed coefficient bound. More explicitly, $$\mathcal F(A_0,4A_0)
 =\frac{112\,528\,360}{10^{21}}+\frac{416}{10^{42}}
 <\frac{12}{10^{14}}.$$ The order-two norm is $|F|_{2,t}=|F(t)|+|F'(t)|+|F''(t)|/2$. Hence the second derivative, unlike the value and first derivative, requires the factor $2$: $$|f_0''-\widetilde f_0''|
 \leq2|f_0-\widetilde f_0|_{2,t}
 <\frac{24}{10^{14}}=2.4\cdot10^{-13}.$$ For the angular function, <a href="#tail:e-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:e-estimate">[tail:e-estimate]</a> similarly yields the entirely rational comparison $$|e''-\widetilde e''|
 \leq2\bigl(16(2\cdot10^6)A_0+32A_0^2\bigr)
 =\frac{128\,000\,000}{10^{21}}+\frac{256}{10^{42}}
 <\frac{13}{10^{14}}=1.3\cdot10^{-13}.$$ The value and first derivative are bounded by their norms without this factor. Thus these rational alternatives preserve the error threshold $10^{-11}$ for all six quantities in <a href="#tail:uniform-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:uniform-error">[tail:uniform-error]</a> on $[b,l]$. For $t\geq l$, the exact continuation <a href="#tail:exterior-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tail:exterior-formula">[tail:exterior-formula]</a>, rather than a uniform absolute error at unbounded radius, remains the applicable statement.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Two possible Voronoi sectors, with the edge normal as angular axis. In the right-hand sector both endpoints are above that axis, so the lower endpoint parameter $α$ is negative even though the sector angle $α + β$ is positive.",
      "figure_id": "fig:signed-sectors",
      "pdf_page": 15,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:signed-sectors",
          "exact_quote": "Two possible Voronoi sectors, with the edge normal as angular\naxis. In the right-hand sector both endpoints are above that axis,\nso the lower endpoint parameter $\\alpha$ is negative even though the\nsector angle $\\alpha+\\beta$ is positive.",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=.95,>=Stealth,font=\\small]\n\\begin{scope}[shift={(0,0)}]\n  \\fill[blue!9] (0,0)--(2.4,-1.05)--(2.4,1.55)--cycle;\n  \\draw[thick] (0,0)--(2.4,-1.05)--(2.4,1.55)--cycle;\n  \\draw[dashed,gray] (0,0)--(2.9,0);\n  \\draw[gray] (2.2,0)--(2.2,.2)--(2.4,.2);\n  \\fill (0,0) circle (1.5pt) node[left] {$p$};\n  \\node[right] at (2.4,-1.05) {$v_-$};\n  \\node[right] at (2.4,1.55) {$v_+$};\n  \\draw[<->] (0,-1.65)--node[above] {$d$}(2.4,-1.65);\n  \\draw[->] (.72,0) arc[start angle=0,end angle=32.86,radius=.72];\n  \\node at (.96,.30) {$\\beta$};\n  \\draw[->] (.64,0) arc[start angle=0,end angle=-23.63,radius=.64];\n  \\node at (.94,-.27) {$-\\alpha$};\n  \\node[align=center] at (1.15,-2.35) {The foot lies on the edge.\\\\$\\alpha,\\beta\\geq0$};\n\\end{scope}\n\\begin{scope}[shift={(6.3,0)}]\n  \\fill[blue!9] (0,0)--(2.4,.65)--(2.4,2)--cycle;\n  \\draw[thick] (0,0)--(2.4,.65)--(2.4,2)--cycle;\n  \\draw[dashed,gray] (0,0)--(2.9,0);\n  \\draw[dashed,gray] (2.4,0)--(2.4,.65);\n  \\draw[gray] (2.2,0)--(2.2,.2)--(2.4,.2);\n  \\fill (0,0) circle (1.5pt) node[left] {$p$};\n  \\node[right] at (2.4,.65) {$v_-$};\n  \\node[right] at (2.4,2) {$v_+$};\n  \\draw[<->] (0,-1.65)--node[above] {$d$}(2.4,-1.65);\n  \\draw[->] (1.20,0) arc[start angle=0,end angle=39.81,radius=1.20];\n  \\node at (1.45,.80) {$\\beta$};\n  \\draw[->] (.70,0) arc[start angle=0,end angle=15.15,radius=.70];\n  \\node at (.78,-.18) {$-\\alpha>0$};\n  \\node[align=center] at (1.15,-2.35) {The foot lies beyond the edge.\\\\$\\alpha<0<\\beta$};\n\\end{scope}\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two geometric diagrams of Voronoi sectors. Left diagram: sector with vertex at site $p$ and base along the vertical edge between endpoints $v_-$ and $v_+$. A horizontal dashed line segment of length $d$ connects $p$ perpendicularly to the edge, with a right-angle indicator at the foot. Rays connect $p$ to $v_+$ with angle $\\beta$ above the horizontal axis, and $p$ to $v_-$ with angle $-\\alpha$ below the axis. Below the diagram are the labels \"The foot lies on the edge.\" and \"$\\alpha, \\beta \\ge 0$\". Right diagram: sector where the edge between $v_-$ and $v_+$ lies entirely above the horizontal axis from $p$. Rays connect $p$ to $v_-$ at angle $-\\alpha > 0$ and $p$ to $v_+$ at angle $\\beta$. A horizontal dashed line segment of length $d$ extends to the line collinear with the edge, ending at a perpendicular foot marked with a right-angle indicator below $v_-$. Below the diagram are the labels \"The foot lies beyond the edge.\" and \"$\\alpha < 0 < \\beta$\"."
    }
  ],
